@@2662660 <h> Gaza and the politics of forgetting <p> As the current war on Gaza has entered its third week , approximately 800 Palestinians have been killed . While Stephen Harper has pledged to remain " steadfast in support of Israel 's right to defend itself , " the Prime Minister 's Office does not appear to be concerned about the mounting death toll in Gaza , or the fact that , as of yesterday , OCHA ( The Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs ) , reported that 33 per cent of civilian deaths are children . Instead , the Conservatives are busy creating websites to fundraise for Israel . <p> This unconcern for Palestinian lives extends to other political parties , as Liberal and NDP leaders have also reiterated Israel 's right to defend itself against aggression , but failed to simultaneously mention the right that Palestinians have to resist colonial occupation . And herein lies the crux of the problem : the latest violence in Gaza is conveniently disconnected from the reality of occupation . <p> After militarily occupying Gaza since 1967 , in 2005 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and withdrew its military to the borders , but it did not end its occupation of an area considerably smaller than the entire Island of Montreal , and one of the most densely packed places on earth . In fact , what Israel did was shift the occupation from a typical settler-colonial model to an atypical and , as some have noted , vertical one . <p> As Lisa Hajjar explains , Israel 's occupation of Gaza continues to the present day because " ( a ) Israel continues to exercise ' effective control ' over this area , ( b ) the conflict that produced the occupation has not ended and ( c ) an occupying state can not unilaterally ( and without international/diplomatic agreement ) transform the international status of occupied territory except , perhaps , if that unilateral action terminates all manner of effective control . " <p> Due to the fact that Israel maintains control over Gaza 's borders , airspace and territorial waters , in addition to water , electricity and phone services , it can not be earnestly claimed that " effective control " has @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ living conditions , and the well-documented historical " de-development " of Gaza , Palestinians in the Strip democratically elected Hamas in 2006 . Almost immediately , Israel punished Palestinians in Gaza by imposing economic and political sanctions . The following year , Israel declared the Gaza Strip a " hostile entity . " <p> This declaration was significant , as it effectively transformed Gaza into a state of exception where normal rights do not apply . Within this liminal space , Palestinian bodies are viewed as obstacles to be destroyed or controlled in the maintenance of the colonial order . <p> In Wasted Lives , Bauman explains how those designated as outside the body politic , often referred to as " others , " are viewed as superfluous , alien bodies " gnawing at the healthy tissues of society and sworn enemies of ' our way of life ' and ' what we stand for . ' " All Palestinians are , therefore , the omnipresent threat , be it " demographic " or otherwise . <p> The historic regulation and fragmentation of Palestinian bodies and land , not only in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ territory , is a necessary strategy to continue Israeli domination and occupation . <p> In Stormy Weather : Katrina and the Politics of Disposability , Giroux reminds us that the body is an " object of power " that " needs to be ' regularized ' , subject to those immaterial means of production that produce ways of life that enlarge the targets of control and regulation . " In the occupied Palestinian territories , life can be administered and regulated in order to ensure that Palestinians continue to be controlled . Through the blockade of Gaza that included the closure of border crossings , limits on provision of fuel and electricity , limits to legal fishing areas , buffer zones along the border with Israel , a no-go area in the North , and severe restrictions on the movement of people , Gaza was transformed into a veritable open-air prison where few had actually committed any crimes . <p> While the blockade was designed to " strangle " and isolate Hamas , it had the opposite effect . Israel 's strategy backfired and Hamas only cemented its control and support @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in March 2008 , that " The policy of isolating Hamas and sanctioning Gaza is bankrupt and , by all conceivable measures , has backfired ... Economic conditions are ruinous , generating anger and despair ... Those intending to undermine Hamas have instead given it an assist . " <p> The terrible conditions in Gaza led the head of UNRWA to remark that " Hungry , unhealthy , angry communities do not make good partners for peace . " <p> The tunnels that Israel has somehow managed to use as a pretext for the current invasion of Gaza ex post facto , are another product of Israel 's skewed logic . When Israel banned the import of basic items into Gaza , including cement , irrigation pipes , tar , plaster , A4 paper and even chocolate , and attempted to reduce the flow of goods to a " humanitarian minimum " in order to put Palestinians " on a diet , but not to make them die of hunger , " they also created the conditions necessary for such desperate attempts at reaching the outside world . <p> It was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of tunnels was transformed into a major commercial enterprise . As Nicholas Pelham notes , the tunnels , became a " key driver of upward mobility and social change , empowering previously marginalized groups .... " <p> John Holmes , the United Nations under secretary general for Humanitarian Affairs and Emergency Relief Coordinator , remarked in 2010 that " the situation without the tunnels would be completely unsustainable . " It is fair to assume , then , that the existence of the tunnels , first discovered by Israel in 1983 , are predicated upon Israel 's perpetual occupation of Gaza . <p> And , while international aid has become necessary for Gaza 's survival , this assistance simultaneously obfuscates Israel 's own legal responsibilities toward the occupied Palestinian population . This means that Israel can bomb Gaza , kill civilians and destroy vital infrastructure without worrying about the financial costs attached to rebuilding . Moreover , as Qarmout and Beland ( 2012 ) note , " ... assistance has , inadvertently and unintentionally , increased Gazans ' dependence on humanitarian aid , impeded economic development , and enabled Israel to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in March 2008 led the head of UNRWA to state that Gaza was on the verge of becoming " the first territory to be intentionally reduced to a state of abject destitution , with the knowledge , acquiescence and -- some would say -- encouragement of the international community . " <p> Within this surreal narrative , the U.S. can pledge $47 million in aid to Gaza while simultaneously funding Israel 's invasion , effectively supplying it with weapons , technology and the political support needed to carry out the destruction it later pays to fix . While U.S. support might currently appear normalized , if Israel is found guilty of war crimes and crimes against humanity , as it was in 2008-2009 , the U.S. , along with countries like Canada who offer unconditional support , should be deemed complicit in the slaughter of innocents . <p> Seven months prior to the death and destruction wrought on Gaza by Operation Cast Lead in 2008-2009 , a coalition of aid agencies and human rights organizations presciently warned " Peace will not be achieved by locking 1.5 million people into a prison @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ policy of isolation will only continue the cycle of increasing poverty and extremism in Gaza rather than bring an end to it . " Given the ongoing occupation and control of Gaza , this warning continues to be relevant , but also conveniently ignored by Israel . <p> It is no surprise that the current invasion of Gaza began shortly after a Hamas-Fatah unity government was formed . The United States ' recent admission that it would work with the new government , greatly concerned Israel , as it knows that any rapprochement between Hamas and Fatah , blessed by the U.S. , could confer international legitimacy on Hamas . This legitimacy is a serious threat to Israel , as it destroys its self-appointed powers of deterrence and removes the longstanding excuse for colonization and occupation of Palestinian territory . As philosopher Giorgio Agamben remarks , " A system that can only function in a state of emergency must preserve the emergency at any cost . " Without the Hamas boogeyman , Israel does n't have a leg to stand on . <p> When Harper says , " The indiscriminate rocket @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ which there is no justification , " we have to recall the history of colonization in Palestine . And when Israel asserts , ad nauseum , its right to defend itself from rocket attacks , we must remember that self-defence is not a viable excuse when you have colonized someone else 's territory for 47 years . <p> In short , the spectacle of invasion should not make us forget about the otherwise torturous banality of Israel 's ongoing occupation . <p> Paul Di Stefano is an educator , activist and researcher from Montreal , Canada .

@@2664956 <h> Muslims share fears of extremism <h> Pew survey shows Islam 's adherents react as negatively to terror groups as folks from other faiths <p> Do you ever wonder what Muslims around the world think of terrorist groups like al-Qaida and Boko Haram ? It turns out that most are just as worried about those groups as everyone else . <p> That 's the finding of a new Pew Research Center survey of people in 14 countries with large Muslim populations . The survey , which polled 14,000 Muslims in April ; and May , asked respondents what they thought of al-Qaida , Boko Haram , the Taliban , Hezbollah and Hamas . It found almost universal negative opinions for all of the groups . Al-Qaida , one of the most notorious of the terrorist groups , was viewed negatively by strong majorities in all 14 countries . <p> " As well-publicized bouts of violence , from civil war to suicide bombings , plague the Middle East , Africa and South Asia , concern about Islamic extremism is high among countries with substantial Muslim populations , " Pew @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , concern about extremism has increased in the past year . " <p> In Lebanon , which shares a border with Syria , 92 per cent of those interviewed said they were worried about Islamic extremism . Concern has also risen in Jordan and Turkey , and in Pakistan , where 66 per cent are concerned about the same thing . <p> The survey was conducted before the radical Islamic State in Iraq and the Levant ( ISIL ) seized large parts of Iraq and Syria , and before the latest round of fighting between Israel and Hamas in the Gaza Strip . At that time , 63 per cent of people living in Gaza had a negative opinion of Hamas , an increase from 54 per cent in 2013 . <p> Canadian Muslim leader Sheema Khan , a longtime columnist for the Globe and Mail , is n't surprised by the survey . <p> Like many other Muslims in Canada , she is " horrified " by extremism conducted in the name of her religion . <p> When asked about extremist groups like ISIL , which are committing acts of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do n't represent what she knows about Islam . <p> " I do n't recognize my faith in anything they are saying , " she says . <p> She recognizes the Islamic ideas and words they use , she says , noting they are the same ones she uses . " But I do n't see anything familiar in them , " she says . <p> As a Canadian , she does n't want that group -- which has called on all Muslims to pledge allegiance to its rule -- to " define us " as an Islamic community in Canada . <p> What worries her is that non-Muslims in Canada might see what ISIL and other extremists are doing and saying , and then think this represents all Muslims . Muslims , she states , are " not marching to the same drummer . " <p> Like the rest of us , Khan feels " just as helpless " as other Canadians to do anything about the situation . <p> What she is pledging to do is to help shape what it means to be a Muslim in Canada . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ constructive Islam , one that is " wholly Canadian " and integrated in Canadian society -- an Islam that finds Muslims working with other Canadians to address issues such as the environment , poverty and injustice . <p> " My life and my family is here , " she says . Canada is " my country . " <p> Closer to home , local Muslim community leader Shahina Siddiqui is also was repelled by the violence in Iraq . <p> Siddiqui 's prayer during Ramadan , which ended in mid-July , was that " the evil doers " of ISIL and other Islamic terrorist groups " will be defeated and peace and harmony will once again prevail " in places like Iraq and Afghanistan , and wherever else there is violence and unrest in the name of her religion . <p> She also prayed for peace " in other parts of the world , and that the world will gain peace and social harmony . @@2667927 <p> Benjamin Netanyahu also added that a demilitarization of Gaza must be part of any future solution in the territory . <p> The full letter that was posted : <p> The current conflict in Israel and Gaza is of great concern to anyone who believes in democracy and human rights . <p> While the world watches in agony , we recognize the need for a ceasefire that leads to long-term security and peace in the region . Israel has the right to defend itself and its citizens against terrorist attacks . <p> At this difficult time , let us remember the values we share with Israel : a vibrant , culturally rich democratic nation committed to maintaining the rights of its citizens , regardless of gender or religion . Israel is an example not only to the region , but the world . <p> Support for Israel abroad makes a difference . <p> As the world continues to hope for a peace that satisfies both Israelis and Palestinians , I 'm proud that British Columbia can be counted as a friend of Israel .

( Associated Press ) ( Associated Press ) <h> External Links <p> ( Note : CBC does not endorse and is not responsible for the content of external links . ) <p> July has been a dangerous month for airline passengers . <p> The month has n't quite ended and there 's already been three major crashes , resulting in 464 deaths , flights to Tel Aviv were cancelled because of safety concerns over the fighting in Gaza and , on Friday , F-16 jets were scrambled to escort a Sunwing Boeing 737 back to Toronto after a passenger allegedly made threats to flight safety . <p> And we still do n't really know what happened to Malaysia Airline Flight MH370 , which @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ setting off an international hunt in the southern reaches of the Indian Ocean . <p> Airlines accidents are rare , and 2013 was , in fact the safest year on record for air travel , measured by the death toll . <p> But 2014 has already broken the improving aviation safety pattern of recent years . Since 1946 , only 2012 had fewer . <p> Only 10 of those fatal incidents were on commercial passenger flights , and matches the lowest number ever , for the fourth time , along with the years 2004 , 2009 and 2012 . <p> The annual average for the first four years this decade was 21.5 air accidents with fatal outcomes . @@2670562 <h> Nationalism , war and the perils of talking tough <p> Prime Minister Stephen Harper , left , and then-Defence Minister Peter MacKay look out from a bunker as the visit a forward operating base in the district of Sperwan Ghar , Afghanistan on Monday , May 30 , 2011 . THE CANADIAN PRESS/Sean Kilpatrick <p> National pride is a tricky thing . It 's a collective emotion that dances on the edge of a razor . At its best , national pride is the source of good works . It inspires art and scientific achievement , charity and solid citizenship . It 's a powerful way to unite a country behind a common goal . <p> But tip it the tiniest bit in the wrong direction , and national pride quickly turns ugly . The start of Vladimir Putin 's third term as president of Russia in 2012 was marked by mass protests and allegations of electoral fraud . Putin had hoped to re-take the presidency with confidence and a strong mandate . Instead , the pressure was on him to solidify his tenuous political position . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a narrative of a Russia beset by enemies , inside and out . Putin told a story of traditional Russians fighting back against both physical and existential threats . <p> As a result , Russia 's LGBT community was cast in the role of bogeyman , a menace to conservative values . The year 2013 was marked by vicious , homophobic hate crimes . It 's impossible to prove a direct cause-and-effect relationship between those attacks and Putin 's castigation of Russia 's sexual minorities . But there 's little doubt that the government line led to heightened tensions and inspired bigots who felt they could act with impunity . <p> At the same time , Putin was positioning himself as a protector of the Russian diaspora . It 's perhaps unsurprising that Putin has tried to play the role of benevolent defender of Russians in Crimea . After the Russian invasion of South Ossetia , Putin 's popularity hit a peak . Russia 's annexation of Crimea led to even more spectacular approval ratings for Putin , and so far they 've shown no sign of falling . <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is n't made up out of whole cloth . Israel is a country that does n't need to look far for existential threats . Its relationship with Hamas , the governing party in the Gaza strip since 2007 , is antagonistic at best , openly violent at worst . <p> And so , when three Israeli teenagers were kidnapped last month , it lit a fuse that , on July 8 of this year , exploded into a full-fledged bombardment of Gaza . <p> Harper 's vision is of a Canada that wages war instead of keeping peace -- a Canada that glorifies its military history but forgets its living veterans . <p> Three days after the teenagers went missing , Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu told a cabinet meeting , " These teenagers were kidnapped and the kidnapping was carried out by Hamas members . We know that for a fact . " After their bodies were discovered on June 30 , Netanyahu tweeted , " They were abducted &amp; murdered in cold blood by human animals , " and quoted a poem by Haim Nahman Bialik : " Vengeance @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not yet created . " <p> Since then , questions have been raised as to why Netanyahu and other members of his cabinet took to the Internet to inflame national sentiment . Evidence has surfaced suggesting the Israeli government was aware early on that there was no hope of bringing the three kidnapped teenagers home alive , but concealed that tragic fact from the public . And while Netanyahu was quick to blame Hamas for the kidnapping , it has since quietly emerged that a lone cell , unconnected to the Hamas leadership , was the real culprit . <p> As of today , almost 1,200 Palestinians , 53 Israeli soldiers and three Israeli civilians have been killed in Operation Protective Edge . It 's hard not to wonder if some of those lives might have been saved had Netanyahu resisted the impulse to stoke the flames of nationalist rage . <p> Again and again , history has shown that when politicians try to turn the angrier form of nationalism into political advantage , they lose control . Most recently , Europe has seen a sharp rise in anti-Semitic violence -- @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ also linked to the rise of far-right nationalist parties like France 's National Front . <p> We should be wary when our own politicians try to exploit certain flavours of nationalism for political gain . There 's nothing wrong with , for example , celebrating Canada 's athletic achievements , as Prime Minster Harper did when his Vancouver Olympics jacket became ubiquitous on the 2011 campaign trail . But Harper has taken to peppering his speeches and policy positions with militaristic bombast . <p> The government spent $30 million trying ( and failing ) to gin up national enthusiasm for the War of 1812 bicentennial . It earmarked another $83 million over the next six years to commemorate Canada 's military history -- never mind that the money might be put to better use supporting veterans in desperate need after they come home . <p> Is that really the Canada we want to be ? Harper 's vision is of a Canada that wages war instead of keeping peace -- a Canada that glorifies its military history but forgets its living veterans . <p> Ultimately , he would have us as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ helps . <p> We can do better . Our country has more to celebrate than jingoistic militarism and whitewashed history . Our politicians should do more than stoke fantasies of Canada as " the best country in the world " . Instead , they should build the policies to get us there . <p> Devon Black is studying law at the University of Victoria . In addition to writing for iPolitics , Devon has worked for the Canadian International Development Agency , Leadership Africa USA and RamRais &amp; Partners . <p> The views , opinions and positions expressed by all iPolitics columnists and contributors are the author 's alone . They do not inherently or expressly reflect the views , opinions and/or positions of iPolitics. 
@@2670621 <h> Kingstonians seek peace in Gaza <p> A July 22 rally organized by Queen 's faculty members saw over 300 people stand outside City Hall in silent solidarity for Gaza . <p> The protestors carried signs and Palestinian flags . An older man walked past and said , " If you feel so strongly , go over there and join them . Fucking terrorists . @@2673254 <p> Benjamin Franklin is credited with the phrase , " An ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure " . This statement has survived over two hundred years because it makes sense , common sense . But then up pops the Canada Revenue Agency like Monty Python 's Spanish Inquisition to inform us all that preventing poverty can not be the aim of Oxfam Canada , one of the most trusted and respected aid agencies in the world . <p> What kind of Orwellian doublespeak is this ? According to Canada 's charity overseers it 's OK to help alleviate poverty but for Oxfam to maintain its not-for-profit status no longer can it aim to " prevent " poverty . This story would have fit so well into Robin Whitaker 's recent Gadfly column " The Trouble with Charity " . <p> To declare the prevention of poverty invalid as a charitable act is farcical , most worthy of a Rick Mercer Rant or a 22 Minutes skit . But it does have serious ramifications . <h> Alleviation Vs AND Prevention <p> How does one distinguish @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ instance , are designed to alleviate hunger . They do nothing to prevent it . According to Revenue Canada food banks provide a valid charitable service . However , anyone lobbying for lower drug costs , increased financial supports or advocating for affordable housing are not worthy of charitable status . Yet even the provincial government has recognized that the best way to reduce poverty is via a Poverty Reduction Strategy that focuses on supports to both alleviate and prevent poverty . It is a development truism that to eradicate poverty one needs to focus on its causes . <p> The same is true globally . Many millions find themselves desperately poor due to a particular natural emergency such as an earthquake ( e.g. Haiti ) or a typhoon ( e.g. The Philippines ) . Other explanations why so many people find themselves in poverty are more political in nature -- for example , the Gaza bombardment or the refugee exodus of Syria . <p> Oxfam has a strong record in responding to such disasters in providing safe water and sanitation to tens of thousands of people , women , men @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 25 emergency crises at a time worldwide . It is from direct experience of responding to and assisting desperately poor people that Oxfam feels duty-bound to raise its voice and to offer solutions that will help prevent future crises . <p> Currently Oxfam staff who live and work in Gaza are distributing emergency food vouchers and providing safe water to nearly 50,000 people who have fled their homes and who are sheltering in schools , churches , mosques and hospitals . Oxfam also operates a mobile healthcare clinic in Gaza . This day to day experience in Gaza has compelled Oxfam to call for a lasting peace and an end to the Israeli blockade : " The international community needs to redouble efforts to achieve a just and lasting peace that brings security and development to all Palestinians and Israelis . Israel must end the blockade on Gaza , which is collectively punishing an entire civilian population . " <h> Overcoming poverty " an act of justice " : Mandela <p> For over 30 years I worked as an educator and campaigner for Oxfam Canada . During my tenure I always @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ causes of poverty that distinguished it from those charities whose financial well-being was focused on exploiting the images of the desperately poor . It was an Oxfam principle that people living in poverty be shown in a dignified way . In seeking donations Oxfam avoided images that elicited pity , but instead Oxfam would focus on those images and stories that would appeal to a sense of solidarity . <p> In 2005 as G8 leaders prepared to meet in Edinburgh , Scotland Oxfam partnered in a new campaign called Make Poverty History , the goal of which was to persuade world leaders to take serious action against global poverty through improved aid , fairer trade rules and putting an end to the debt that was impoverishing generation after generation , denying them access to schools and health clinics . <p> Photo by Justin Brake . <p> The campaign was launched by the recently retired president of South Africa , Nelson Mandela . In his own inimitable way Mandela reminded us that charity would never end poverty . " Overcoming poverty is not a gesture of charity . It is an act @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ human right , the right to dignity and a decent life . While poverty persists , there is no true freedom ... Like Slavery and Apartheid , poverty is not natural . It is man-made and it can be overcome and eradicated by the actions of human beings . " <p> For Mandela poverty was a social crime and as such we needed to speak out , stand up and take action against this very injustice . Such thinking can also be found in the writing of Paulo Friere , a Brazilian educator who worked closely with the poor of his country . Friere saw a distinction between false and true generosity . <p> " True generosity consists precisely in fighting to destroy the causes which nourish false charity . False charity constrains the fearful and subdued , the " rejects of life , " to extend their trembling hands . True generosity lies in striving so that these hands -- whether of individuals or entire peoples -- need be extended less and less in supplication , so that more and more they become human hands which work and , working @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ legacy <p> It is such thinking that is at the heart of Oxfam 's humanitarian , development and campaigning efforts . Oxfam is a rights-based organization that is committed to the eradication of poverty . While I no longer work for Oxfam I feel confident in saying that Oxfam will continue its humanitarian work of aiding people who find themselves in all forms of distress . Oxfam will continue to support partners who are fighting to have their human rights recognized and enforced ; women owning land , children attending school , workers treated fairly are all part of this mix . <p> Oxfam will also continue to join with others in demanding that world leaders own up to their obligations on such issues as aid , climate change and respect for human rights . And Oxfam will continue to campaign and advocate on issues such as the Robin Hood Tax , a small financial tax , as a source of funds that could go along to helping alleviate and prevent poverty . <p> As for the Canada Revenue Agency , it will continue to administer the tax laws for Canada @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ up with the thinking of Benjamin Franklin and accept that an ounce of prevention is worth more than a pound of cure . <p> Bill Hynd worked for Oxfam Canada as an educator and campaigner for 30 years . He is currently the co-chair of the NL Social Justice Cooperative . <p> Editor 's note : If you would like to respond to this or any article on TheIndependent.ca , or if you would like to address an issue we have n't yet covered , we welcome letters to the editor and consider each of them for publication in our Letters section . @@2673332 <h> Yakov M. Rabkin : Israeli Jews can criticize Israel . So why ca n't we ? <p> Even as the war in Gaza continues , Israeli attitudes about the murder of three Jewish teenagers in June , which helped spark the conflict , vary widely . <p> Some blame the spread of settlements and their promoters , arguing that Palestinians have endured many tragedies . Some even blame the Zionist nature of the state of Israel . Many others , on the contrary , insist that Palestinians have to be taught another harsh lesson , some even arguing for their wholesale " transfer " to some as-yet-unidentified Arab country . This diversity of viewpoint , at times of trial , is impressive . <p> When I was in Israel earlier this year , I saw a demonstration of many thousands of Haredi ( ultra-orthodox ) Jews protesting against compulsory army service , one the pillars of the Israeli identity . I had gone to Israel on a book tour , to promote the Hebrew adaptation of my study of Jewish opposition to Zionism . ( The original @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has since been translated into a dozen languages . ) <p> The Haredi protest I witnessed was just the tip of the iceberg . There are many elements in Israel that refuse to join in with the mostly secular mainstream of the country 's society . What triggered the protest was a decision of the Israeli government to phase out exemptions for Yeshiva students and to oblige them to serve in the army . This would put an end to the historic agreement concluded in 1948 between David Ben Gurion , Israel 's first Prime Minister , and non-Zionist rabbis . <h> Related <p> Ben Gurion was an irreligious man who expected that Haredi Judaism would die out . He believed that the Haredim , with their black kaftans and long beards , were vestiges of pre-modern Europe who would assimilate to the new secular society in Israel . His prediction has proven wrong . More than a million Israelis now consider themselves Haredim , and they remain aloof from the country 's secular majority . This causes resentment , particularly in regard to their refusal to share the burden of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ underlying causes of this estrangement . <p> This helps explain why my book attracted attention from a broad range of Israeli media . Even before I left for Israel , a journalist from the major Israeli daily Yediot Aharonot called me on the telephone and later published the interview on a full two-page spread . His colleagues from Shaa Tova and Bekehila , weeklies catering to religious Jews , each published in-depth interviews . I was interviewed by several other publications , including Time Out , a Tel Aviv weekly catering to secular Israelis , and Arutz 7 , run by settlers in the West Bank . Two TV stations invited me to discuss Jewish opposition to Zionism . This broad gamut of Israelis appreciated my concern about the future of their society and my desire to open up a debate they vitally need . They were not afraid to read denunciations of Zionism quoted and analysed in my book . <p> Western organizations that claim to be pro-Israel routinely stifle debate and denounce as anti-Semites those ( like me ) who persist <p> This stands in stark contrast with the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in Canada . The book , albeit short-listed for the Governor General Award , was mostly ignored , and I was ostracised . Only now do I realize that I acted " too Israeli . " <p> I originally began studying Jewish anti-Zionism during a sabbatical in Israel with a group of ardent Zionists , some of them West Bank settlers . This is why , once back in Montreal , I thought I could discuss and criticize Israel without fear or circumspection . Many others try to do the same , but Western organizations that claim to be pro-Israel routinely stifle debate and denounce as anti-Semites those ( like me ) who persist . This is unhealthy and unnecessary . <p> It also creates a misleading image of Israel as an insecure society shunning uncomfortable truths . My recent experience in Israel has proved once again that we must be able to discuss and criticise Israel just as we would any other country . It is time for us to learn from Israelis and allow for free and open debate about their country . <p> National Post <p> The author @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ? al . The English version of his book is entitled A Threat from Within . His most recent book is Comprendre l ' ? tat d'Isra ? l , id ? ologie , religion et soci ? t ? .

@@2675850 <p> Dominique Moisi is a professor at L'Institut d ' ? tudes politiques de Paris and a senior adviser at the French Institute for International Affairs ( IFRI ) . <p> PARIS <p> " Death to the Jews ! " In Paris and other French cities , the hate-filled words ring out . Attacks on synagogues have taken place for the first time since the Dreyfus Affair at the end of the 19th century . In suburban areas near Paris , such as the town of Sarcelles , known for its climate of religious and ethnic tolerance , groups of young people have deliberately targeted Jewish properties . <p> Faced with the spectacular rise of anti-immigrant populism in France , and now with anti-Zionist demonstrations ( which often coincide with an updated version of anti-Semitism ) , the French Jewish community is anguished and puzzled . Some of its members are quietly asking themselves whether there is a future for them in the land of human rights . <p> French Jews are rediscovering the dual trauma that they experienced during the 20th century : the death-camp deportations of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ its independence in 1962 . It is to be expected that these episodes colour -- and tend to exacerbate -- the emotions of the present . <p> French descendants of Eastern European Jews have not yet fully come to terms with a continent -- including Vichy France -- that they still associate with the Holocaust , whereas Jews from the Maghreb tend to resent the fact that even in France , they remain surrounded by " Arabs . " Indeed , a significant portion of the Jewish community in the south of France votes for the far-right National Front , which , under the leadership of Marine Le Pen , has concentrated its xenophobia on Muslims . <p> In this tense setting , it comes as no surprise that the question of whether anti-Semitism has returned to France , after a seven-decade hiatus , is making international headlines . British and American media reports have made comparisons to the Nazi era , with some even referring , in the aftermath of attacks on French synagogues , to a French Kristallnacht . <p> Such hyperbole must be firmly rejected , for it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of Vichy France 's collaboration with Nazi Germany . After the Gestapo arrested my father in Nice in 1943 , he was escorted by French gendarmes to the transit camp at Drancy , in the Paris suburbs , before being deported to Auschwitz . In 2014 , by contrast , the French state defends the synagogues and denounces any form of anti-Semitism . <p> But even if the French state is not anti-Semitic , anti-Semitism does exist in France -- and probably more so recently than in the postwar period . And the worsening situation in the Middle East has , of course , played a key role in this , particularly the shocking images from Gaza . The asymmetrical war being waged there by Israel seems disproportionate to a majority of world opinion , not just Arabs and Muslims . <p> To be sure , no state can passively accept rocket attacks on its cities . And , yes , Hamas deliberately chooses to place its military arsenal in highly populated areas under the involuntary protective shield of innocent civilians -- or those Israeli officials sometimes refer to , with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> But the strategy of terror used by the Israeli authorities to deter further attacks or to restore a temporary " quiet " has been costly not only in terms of Palestinian lives lost and Israeli soldiers killed ; it has also contributed to the deterioration of the security of Jews around the world . In France , too , many of them express -- often quietly -- both their deep love for what Israel is and their deep concern for what Israel is now doing . <p> It is one thing to say that the Middle East conflict should not be exported to France . It is another to recognize the inevitable impact of images of dead Palestinian women and children on communities in France that feel close to Palestine the way that Jews feel close to Israel . If the images from Gaza seem to resonate so much in France , it is partly a matter of sheer numbers : the largest Muslim community in Europe faces the largest Jewish community in Europe . <p> But it is not only a question of numbers . The young thugs who @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ unemployed and frustrated . They vent their rage at a system that does not integrate them . They even resent the Republic 's commemoration of Jews ' suffering during the Second World War . For them , past horror is abstract ; only the present horror can be felt . <p> The encounter between the images of today 's Middle East and the discontent of Muslim minorities ( sometimes influenced by radical fundamentalist ideologies ) should not be allowed to obscure traditional French anti-Semitism , white and bourgeois , which still lingers and is never far beneath the surface . Thanks to the Internet , it , too , has begun to emerge more often . <p> But the French state does what it has to do to repress and contain anti-Semitism . Comparisons to Nazi-era Europe do nothing to reassure a community that , despite all of the major historical differences between then and now , can not quite shake the feeling that it is dancing on the rim of a volcano . 
@@2675871 <h> The children of Gaza need the world 's help <p> A displaced Palestinian boy looks out from a makeshift tent at the al-Shifa hospital in Gaza City where many Palestinians have have taken refuge after fleeing attacks in the Shejaiya neighbourhood of the city , on July 31 , 2014 . The London Daily Telegraph has published a graphic of the names and ages of 132 children who have been killed , in an attempt to humanize them . The youngest of them inhabited this world for only three months . It is surreal going through the list of these distant , unheralded children : Ammar Ahmad Jodeh , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Malaka , 6 ; Raneen Jawdat , 1 ; Fares Jom'a Al Tarabeen , 3 months -- it goes on and on . <p> Some of the children died in their mother 's or father 's arms or in their sleep . Others at the dinner table and still others on playgrounds or where their parents felt were safe UN shelters . One family of seven fled a neighbourhood that was being bombarded by Israeli fire , to another they thought was safer , only to be blown up by a shell . The father , mother and five children all perished . Four boys aged 9-11 were playing on a beach when a blast from an Israeli ship killed them . Moments earlier , they were playing soccer with journalists . They were not human shields , and did n't deserve to die . <p> So far in the Gaza war , more than 1,370 Palestinians have been killed , at least 75 per cent of them civilian . About 7,000 are injured . On the Israeli side , 56 soldiers , two civilians and a foreign worker have also @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ than Hamas militants , the UN and several charities say . More than half the children are under 12 years old . <p> The horror of Palestinian children is real , and yet some gloss over it . Few are brave or compassionate enough to condemn without equivocation the wanton killing of so many innocents . If more than 200 children were dying anywhere in the world but the Palestinian territories , the world will not stand for it . But because it is happening in Gaza , people rationalize the killings or stay silent . <p> The prevailing narrative is that Hamas started this war by firing rockets at Israeli citizens , and Israel ca n't hold back . It simply has no choice . Of course Israel has a choice , and it chose this path with clear-eyed resolve . When Israel chooses to line up tanks which fire into the urban ghetto of 1.8 million people that Gaza is , it knows that it is going to kill a lot of innocent people , including women and children . When Israeli F-16 warplanes fire missiles into this crowded @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ doubt what the consequences will be . It is not for nothing that the charity Save the Children has called the Gaza operation " a war on children . " Sure , Israel has a right to defend itself . But how does the death of so many children make Israel safe ? What about the other children who are scarred by this horror ? Will they grow up to be ambassadors of peace ? <p> Obviously , Hamas is not blameless . Its rockets and mortar fire have so far claimed three civilian lives in Israel in this latest conflict , three lives too many , Israelis will say . Fair enough . But what about the lives of 200 children and other innocent people ? Israel of course maintains that civilians are dying because Hamas is using them as human shields . Hamas bears some responsibility because rockets have been fired from residential areas . But it stretches credulity to say that the civilian casualties are all Hamas 's doing . @@2675987 <h> VISUAL VIEWPOINTS : Emily Falencki transforms the sting of grief <p> The Mira River , 2012 , by Emily Falencki is in her solo show at the Art Gallery of Nova Scotia to Sept. 7 . <p> Emily Falencki never detaches from the news , as painful as it is . <p> She connects her personal world to global conflicts from the Second World War to the present day in tragic , profound and beautiful paintings now on exhibit at the Art Gallery of Nova Scotia to Sept. 7 . <p> " It 's simply about grief and pain . That 's what unites us . Everybody can relate to that , " she said in a phone interview from Cape Breton . <p> Falencki , having her first solo show at the gallery , hopes her paintings will make people slow down to more deeply consider the images of tragedy that fly by on TV or the Internet . " The way we get the images through the news and media we 've been desensitized , " she said . <p> She also wants to give @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a history of when you paint something , it becomes important . " <p> Falencki 's paintings depict women , men and children who are grieving or wounded or dead . She paints them vaguely and on a human scale so " it 's almost as if it 's a mirror , " she said . " It could be your son or your mother . It 's not specifically one story , it 's everybody 's story . " <p> Her newest series is based on her grandmother 's wartime letters to her mother , husband and son , letters that Falencki received recently after her grandmother 's death . <p> In 1940 , her grandmother escaped from Poland to the United States , leaving her mother in the Warsaw Ghetto , where she was killed in 1943 . <p> The wartime story " was told often , " says the New York-born , Nova Scotia-based artist . " As soon as I as old enough to go , my grandmother took me to Auschwitz because she believed it was important to see that . I was 15 . " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ their stamps , including one with a swastika , as a surface for her figures . " It 's the story of being a refugee or an immigrant and the images tell the same story . " <p> The people are members of Falencki 's family , from her European great-grandfather to her own children , as well as contemporary images from the news of people , including Syrian widows . <p> Falencki paints several images from Syria , including an iconic wartime image of a father holding his dead or wounded child . " It 's just that is happening right now , " says Falencki , who is sure she 'll work with images from the current Gaza-Israel conflict . <p> " The whole point of the work is to try not to pick and choose . One conflict is not more relevant than another . " <p> She started this series in 2003 . " I was in graduate school in Dublin so I was studying in Ireland and I immediately became interested in the conflict . <p> " I grew up in New York City and it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ had that experience of watching that on TV . It made me suddenly have this extreme awareness of the way we look at war and the way we watch things . <p> " People do n't pay attention unless they relate in some way ; they have a historical or familial connection . But why is it we turn off ? " <p> Falencki , whose husband is from Cape Breton , also explores local tragedies . The painting , The Mira River , deals with the 2012 murder of Laura Catherine Jessome . <p> It 's the first painting in which Falencki told two stories at once . " There 's a girl , a beautiful smiling girl , in the background , who was a young woman who was found in a hockey bag on the Mira River . She was murdered violently ... I could n't tell that tragic story in her face . The only image I could find was a beautiful young girl before anything bad happened . I struggled and I scraped it down . I do that a lot , scrape it down and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ twinned a vivid image of a Syrian father screaming with the girl 's barely visible soft and lovely face . " It 's as if he is shouting out and telling her story . " <p> Falencki paints on a smooth surface of gesso mixed with glue and chalk , then sanded . " It is skin-like and it has these pores that suck up the paint and you can erase , you can sand it where things are disappearing and coming forward . " <p> People always ask Falencki how can she bear to deal with this subject matter . " It 'd be more painful to pretend it is n't happening . That is my job -- to explore what is happening in the world . " <p> " It 's a natural thing for me to be sad when there are horrible things happening all over . I also have a lot of joy in my life . I do n't do it 12 hours a day . "

Almost every solider left from here . " <p> Days before the anniversary on Aug. 4 , members of the Halifax Citadel Regimental Association were parading through the grounds , dressed in World War I-era uniforms . <p> Upstairs in the Army Museum , a scale replica of the Vimy Ridge memorial in France dominated a room devoted to Canada 's World War I history . <p> " People are impressed by it , " said Jim Jollota , a volunteer , pointing to the graceful spires of the memorial . " The response we 've gotten has been great . " <p> One of the key figures in organizing all World War I activities at the Hill is Hal Thompson of Parks Canada . <p> He said the anniversary has particular significance in the context of ongoing armed conflicts in Gaza and Ukraine . <p> While total war on the scale of World War I is unlikely today , Thompson said that does n't mean that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the War of 1812 , which we did a couple years ago , people know more about World War I , " he said . " They know about Flanders Field and trench warfare at least . " <p> With commemorative events scheduled over the next four years , many people will know a lot more about World War I than they do now - and it will all start with an hour in the dark .
